\section{Quantum statistical mechanics}
\label{page:quantum-statistical-mechanics}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
A quantum statistical mechanics can be read as a quantum construction: the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis fixes the admissible state space; a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, a quantum statistical mechanics is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or density matrix, observable operator or matrix, and spectrum or eigenvalue. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. \section{Spin (physics)}
\label{page:spin-physics}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
A spin (physics) can be read as a quantum construction: the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis fixes the admissible state space; a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, a spin (physics) is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through wave function or quantum state, Hamiltonian or observable operator, and eigenvalue or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through commutator or uncertainty relation. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout,

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Spin (physics) contributes a topic-native constructor role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a state-carrier move: it specifies what mathematical object carries prediction.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. State terms: wave function and quantum state. Context/domain terms: context.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Operator terms: Hamiltonian. Protocol or update terms: unitary evolution, projection or measurement update, or path integral weighting.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Compatibility or closure terms: commutator, uncertainty relation, or non-commuting transformations. These determine which questions, states, or updates are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Readout terms: eigenvalue, energy level, or measurement outcome. These name the outcome labels, projectors, amplitudes, or records used for testing.
\item \textbf{Check}. A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
\begin{align*}
B \longmapsto \rho_B \quad \text{(context specifies an admissible state)}\\
\rho_t = U_t \rho_B U_t^\dagger \quad \text{(unitary evolution from preparation to readout)}\\
O = \sum_i \lambda_i P_i,\quad p_i=\operatorname{Tr}(P_i\rho_t) \quad \text{(spectral probability measure)}\\
[O_1,O_2]\neq 0 \quad \text{(incompatible observables: no common sharp basis)}
\end{align*}

\subsection*{What Remains Stable}
\begin{itemize}
\item the rule connecting prepared states, observables, and spectral probability measures across wave, matrix, path-integral, circuit, or field notation
\item the operator-to-spectrum relation: admissible observations are represented through eigenvalues, projections, modes, or outcome channels
\item the dependence of admissible readout on measurement context or boundary condition
\item the non-commuting compatibility structure, which survives changes of representation
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{What Changes With Realization}
\begin{itemize}
\item the name of the carrier: particle, wave, field, qubit, or excitation
\item where time dependence is represented: on the state, on the operator, or in a path weight
\item the coordinate system, basis, or geometric picture used to display the same relation
\item the physical implementation of detector, boundary, preparation, or readout
\end{itemize}

\item A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.

\section{Schrödinger's cat}
\label{page:schr-dinger-s-cat}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
a schrödinger's cat modifies the interpretation of the probability/readout layer while preserving the formal quantum dynamics.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
a schrödinger's cat acts on the readout layer of the quantum constructor. The formal ingredients remain the state assignment, the operator or measurement being applied, and the Born-rule map from projectors to probabilities. What changes is the status assigned to those ingredients: for this topic, the state or probability is treated through the agent, measurement context, or interpretive stance attached to the formalism. The page should therefore be read as a statement about the interpretation of state, probability, update, or recorded outcome while the Hamiltonian, spectral resolution, and commutator structure remain the formal reference layer. \chapter{Generator: lawful change before readout}

\begin{claimbox}

Hamiltonians, unitary maps, equations of motion, and path weights describe the lawful transport of the state before a question is resolved.

\end{claimbox}

\section{Why This Branch Exists}

Time evolution is a transport problem over states.  The Hamiltonian and path integral are two views of the same generator role rather than unrelated formalisms.

\section{Unitary operator}
\label{page:unitary-operator}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Unitary operator is the reversible-map constructor: it changes the state while preserving inner products and probabilities.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
Unitary maps are the admissible reversible transformations of closed-system quantum theory. They preserve normalization and carry projective geometry of the state space through time or controlled operations. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, spectral profile.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Unitary operator contributes a lawful state-transport role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a lawful-transport move: it identifies what changes the state before readout.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. A state vector, density operator, wavefunction, field state, or register on a specified domain.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Hamiltonian, unitary map, channel generator, action, constraint, or differential operator that transports the state.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Self-adjointness, complete positivity, trace preservation, gauge constraints, and boundary/domain conditions decide whether the evolution is legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Time-dependent probabilities, spectra, transition amplitudes, conserved quantities, or response functions.
\item \textbf{Check}. The generator must predict the observed evolution while preserving the relevant normalization, positivity, symmetry, or conservation constraint.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Standard constructor skeleton: reversible state transformation and inner-product preservation.
\begin{align*}
U^\dagger U=UU^\dagger=I\\
\ket{\psi'}=U\ket{\psi}\\
\langle\psi'|\phi'\rangle=\langle\psi|\phi\rangle
\end{align*}

\section{Path integral}

Path integral is an alternate generator constructor: transition amplitudes are obtained by summing phase weights over histories.

\section{Creation and annihilation operators}
\label{page:creation-and-annihilation-operators}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Creation and annihilation operators are sector-changing operators: they add or remove one quantum from a mode and make many-body or field descriptions executable.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
The page is about the algebraic move that changes occupation number. Creation raises the population of a mode, annihilation lowers it, and the commutation or anticommutation rule determines the statistics. The number operator gives the spectral readout. N_i=a_i^\dagger a_i
\end{align*}

\label{page:schr-dinger-equation}

\subsection*{Central Claim}

\subsection*{Formal Role}
The Schrödinger equation is not a measurement rule. It is the generator step of the quantum constructor. It evolves the predictive carrier while preserving normalization when the Hamiltonian is self-adjoint. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Schrödinger equation contributes a lawful state-transport role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a lawful-transport move: it identifies what changes the state before readout.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. A state vector, density operator, wavefunction, field state, or register on a specified domain.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Hamiltonian, unitary map, channel generator, action, constraint, or differential operator that transports the state.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Self-adjointness, complete positivity, trace preservation, gauge constraints, and boundary/domain conditions decide whether the evolution is legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Time-dependent probabilities, spectra, transition amplitudes, conserved quantities, or response functions.
\item \textbf{Check}. The generator must predict the observed evolution while preserving the relevant normalization, positivity, symmetry, or conservation constraint.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Standard constructor skeleton: Hamiltonian transport and norm preservation.
\begin{align*}
i\hbar\,\partial_t\ket{\psi(t)}=H\ket{\psi(t)}\\
\ket{\psi(t)}=U(t)\ket{\psi(0)},\qquad U(t)=e^{-iHt/\hbar}\\
\frac{d}{dt}\langle\psi(t)|\psi(t)\rangle=0\quad (H=H^\dagger)
\end{align*}

\section{Quantum stochastic calculus}
\label{page:quantum-stochastic-calculus}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
A quantum stochastic calculus can be read as a quantum construction: the potential, domain, initial condition, or boundary condition fixes the admissible state space; the Hamiltonian, whose exponential gives unitary time evolution defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, a quantum stochastic calculus is described by a wave function or density operator defined on the Hilbert space allowed by the system's domain. The physical question is represented by the Hamiltonian, whose exponential gives unitary time evolution; the experimental or mathematical setting is the potential, domain, initial condition, or boundary condition. The observable content is obtained from the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of the relevant observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or wave function, Hamiltonian or commutator, and mode or eigenvalue. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through commutator or non-commuting observables. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state

\section{Spectral theory}
\label{page:spectral-theory}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Spectral theory can be read as a quantum construction: the potential, domain, initial condition, or boundary condition fixes the admissible state space; the Hamiltonian, whose exponential gives unitary time evolution defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, spectral theory is described by a wave function or density operator defined on the Hilbert space allowed by the system's domain. The physical question is represented by the Hamiltonian, whose exponential gives unitary time evolution; the experimental or mathematical setting is the potential, domain, initial condition, or boundary condition. The observable content is obtained from the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of the relevant observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or wave function, matrix or Hamiltonian, and eigenvalue or spectrum. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through commutator or uncertainty relation. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution,

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Spectral theory contributes a topic-native constructor role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a question-selection move: it identifies the spectrum or answer set being read.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. State terms: quantum state, wave function, or density operator. Context/domain terms: basis.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Operator terms: matrix. Protocol or update terms: unitary evolution, projection or measurement update, or path integral weighting.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Compatibility or closure terms: commutator, uncertainty relation, or non-commuting transformations. These determine which questions, states, or updates are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Readout terms: eigenvalue and spectrum. These name the outcome labels, projectors, amplitudes, or records used for testing.
\item \textbf{Check}. A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
\begin{align*}
B \longmapsto \rho_B \quad \text{(context specifies an admissible state)}\\
\rho_t = U_t \rho_B U_t^\dagger \quad \text{(unitary evolution from preparation to readout)}\\
O = \sum_i \lambda_i P_i,\quad p_i=\operatorname{Tr}(P_i\rho_t) \quad \text{(spectral probability measure)}\\
[O_1,O_2]\neq 0 \quad \text{(incompatible observables: no common sharp basis)}
\end{align*}

\chapter{Readout rule: how answers become probabilities}

\begin{claimbox}

Measurement connects a state and an observable to recorded frequencies.  Projection, POVMs, Born weights, and collapse language are alternative ways of presenting this state-to-spectrum readout step.

\end{claimbox}

\section{Why This Branch Exists}

Measurement is best placed after observables and spectra: it is the rule that turns spectral resolution into recorded probability, not the mystical starting point of the theory.

\section{POVM}
\label{page:povm}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
POVM is the generalized-readout constructor: outcome effects need not be orthogonal projectors.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
POVMs separate the probability readout from the idealized projection assumption. They are the natural mechanism for noisy, coarse-grained, indirect, or open-system measurements. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure.

\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. A state vector or density operator together with the measurement context in which outcome channels are defined.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. A projection-valued measure, POVM, update map, or instrument map connecting state to record.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Outcome probabilities must be positive, normalized, and tied to a specified readout map rather than to informal observer language.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Born probabilities, detector records, post-measurement states, ensemble frequencies, or decision probabilities.
\item \textbf{Check}. The interpretation is constrained by whether it changes the probability rule, the update rule, the detector model, or only the language used for them.
\end{itemize}

\end{align*}

\section{Quantum jump}
\label{page:quantum-jump}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
A quantum jump can be read as a quantum construction: the measurement basis and experimental arrangement fixes the admissible state space; the self-adjoint observable being asked of that state defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}

The physical question is represented by the self-adjoint observable being asked of that state; the experimental or mathematical setting is the measurement basis and experimental arrangement. The observable content is obtained from the observable's spectral projectors and the Born probabilities assigned to them. \end{align*}

\chapter{Compatibility limit: what cannot be jointly sharp}

\begin{claimbox}

The non-classical part of the theory appears when two otherwise legal questions do not compose into one common sharp question.  Commutators, uncertainty relations, contextuality, Bell tests, and entanglement live here.

\end{claimbox}

\section{Why This Branch Exists}

The non-classical core appears as failure of joint sharpness.  Entanglement, Bell phenomena, and uncertainty are different faces of this compatibility structure.

\section{Bell's theorem}
\label{page:bell-s-theorem}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Bell's theorem is a compatibility/locality stress test: quantum correlations violate bounds satisfied by local hidden-variable assignments.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
Bell's theorem is not a page about a mysterious object. It is a falsifier for a classical joint-assignment model of measurement outcomes. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, spectral profile.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Bell's theorem contributes a compatibility or joint-readout role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a compatibility move: it asks which otherwise legal questions cannot share one sharp answer set.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. One state space or a multipartite state space on which several questions can be asked.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Two or more observables, contexts, correlation operators, or hidden-variable assignments being compared.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Commutators, uncertainty bounds, contextuality constraints, or Bell-type inequalities decide which joint assignments are possible.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Joint spectra, correlations, inequality violations, uncertainty products, or incompatible outcome statistics.
\item \textbf{Check}. The non-classical content appears only if the incompatible questions cannot be replaced by one common sharp classical assignment.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Standard constructor skeleton: CHSH inequality and quantum violation bound.
\begin{align*}
|E(a,b)+E(a,b')+E(a',b)-E(a',b')|\le 2\\
\text{quantum prediction can reach }2\sqrt2
\end{align*}

\section{Quantum entanglement}
\label{page:quantum-entanglement}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Quantum entanglement is the non-factorization constructor: a composite state can carry correlations not reducible to independent subsystem states.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
Entanglement belongs to composition and compatibility. It appears when the tensor-product state cannot be written as a product or mixture of local states, producing correlations that stress classical separability. \rho_A=\operatorname{Tr}_B\ket{\Psi}\bra{\Psi}
\end{align*}

\section{Commutator}
\label{page:commutator}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Commutator is the incompatibility constructor: it measures the failure of two transformations or questions to be freely exchanged.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
The commutator is the algebraic source of many non-classical restrictions. If two observables do not commute, they generally cannot be resolved in one common sharp basis. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, non-commuting compatibility limits; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Commutator contributes a compatibility or joint-readout role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a compatibility move: it asks which otherwise legal questions cannot share one sharp answer set.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. A common state space on which two transformations, observables, or questions are both defined.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. The ordered products AB and BA, compared through the obstruction [A,B]=AB-BA.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. A nonzero commutator marks an order-dependence or compatibility limit; a zero commutator permits a common sharp refinement only when the remaining spectral conditions hold.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Compatibility tests, uncertainty bounds, common eigenspaces, or canonical commutation relations.
\item \textbf{Check}. The mechanism is supported only when changing operator order changes the algebraic or statistical prediction.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Standard constructor skeleton: order obstruction and canonical commutation.
\begin{align*}
[A,B]=AB-BA\\
[A,B]=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad \text{common spectral refinement may exist}\\
[x,p]=i\hbar
\end{align*}

\subsection*{What Remains Stable}
\begin{itemize}

\section{Uncertainty principle}
\label{page:uncertainty-principle}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Uncertainty principle is a compatibility-limit theorem: non-commuting observables impose lower bounds on joint sharpness.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
Uncertainty is not detector imperfection. It is a structural consequence of state variance and non-commuting observables. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Uncertainty principle contributes a compatibility or joint-readout role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a compatibility move: it asks which otherwise legal questions cannot share one sharp answer set.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. One state space or a multipartite state space on which several questions can be asked.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Two or more observables, contexts, correlation operators, or hidden-variable assignments being compared.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Commutators, uncertainty bounds, contextuality constraints, or Bell-type inequalities decide which joint assignments are possible.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Joint spectra, correlations, inequality violations, uncertainty products, or incompatible outcome statistics.
\item \textbf{Check}. The non-classical content appears only if the incompatible questions cannot be replaced by one common sharp classical assignment.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Standard constructor skeleton: variance bound from commutator structure.
\begin{align*}
\Delta_\rho A\,\Delta_\rho B\ge \frac12\left|\operatorname{Tr}(\rho[A,B])\right|\\
\Delta x\,\Delta p\ge\frac{\hbar}{2}
\end{align*}

the Einstein–Podolsky–Rosen paradox acts on the readout layer of the quantum constructor. The formal ingredients remain the state assignment, the operator or measurement being applied, and the Born-rule map from projectors to probabilities. \chapter{Boundary realization: how effects appear}

\begin{claimbox}

Many named quantum effects are boundary realizations of the same construction.  A potential, barrier, box, cavity, detector, or medium changes the allowed spectral channels without changing the basic prediction problem.

\end{claimbox}

\section{Why This Branch Exists}

Named effects such as tunnelling and particle-in-a-box are boundary realizations of the state-operator-readout construction.

\section{Potential well}
\label{page:potential-well}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
A potential well can be read as a quantum construction: the measurement basis and experimental arrangement fixes the admissible state space; the self-adjoint observable being asked of that state defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, a potential well is described by a prepared quantum state before the measurement. The physical question is represented by the self-adjoint observable being asked of that state; the experimental or mathematical setting is the measurement basis and experimental arrangement. The observable content is obtained from the observable's spectral projectors and the Born probabilities assigned to them. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through wave function or state vector, Hamiltonian or observable operator, and spectrum or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through commutator or uncertainty relation. \section{Quantum optics}
\label{page:quantum-optics}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Quantum optics can be read as a quantum construction: the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis fixes the admissible state space; a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, quantum optics is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through wavefunction or wave function, Hamiltonian or observable operator, and spectrum or eigenvalue. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through commutator or uncertainty relation. \section{Quantum tunnelling}
\label{page:quantum-tunnelling}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Quantum tunnelling is a boundary-realization constructor: a state has nonzero transmission through a classically forbidden region.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
Tunnelling shows that the realization layer matters. A potential barrier changes the admissible wave solutions and produces transmission even where classical kinetic energy would be negative. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Quantum tunnelling contributes a boundary-shaped spectrum role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a realization move: it changes the domain, boundary, geometry, or interface in which the operator acts.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. A Hilbert space with a selected domain, potential, interface, asymptotic channel, cavity, well, or boundary condition.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. A Hamiltonian, wave operator, transfer operator, or scattering map whose domain is changed by the boundary.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Boundary conditions and matching conditions determine allowed states, resonances, transmission amplitudes, and spectra.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Eigenvalues, resonances, tunnelling probabilities, phase shifts, reflection/transmission amplitudes, or scattering data.
\item \textbf{Check}. Changing the boundary should change the spectrum or amplitude in the predicted way, while the free or asymptotic limit is recovered when the boundary is removed.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Standard constructor skeleton: barrier-domain Schrödinger equation and WKB transmission.

T\sim \exp\!\left(-2\int_{x_1}^{x_2}\sqrt{\frac{2m(V(x)-E)}{\hbar^2}}\,dx\right)\\
-\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\psi''(x)+V(x)\psi(x)=E\psi(x)
\end{align*}

\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. State terms: wave function. Context/domain terms: potential.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Operator terms: Hamiltonian, observable operator, or generator. Protocol or update terms: unitary evolution, projection or measurement update, or path integral weighting.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Compatibility or closure terms: commutator, uncertainty relation, or non-commuting transformations. These determine which questions, states, or updates are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Readout terms: eigenvalue, energy level, or measurement outcome. These name the outcome labels, projectors, amplitudes, or records used for testing.

B \longmapsto \rho_B \quad \text{(context specifies an admissible state)}\\

[O_1,O_2]\neq 0 \quad \text{(incompatible observables: no common sharp basis)}
\end{align*}

\subsection*{Central Claim}

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, a quantum metamaterial is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or wave function, Hamiltonian or observable operator, and eigenvalue or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. \chapter{Many-mode extension: fields, particles, and scaling}

\begin{claimbox}

Quantum field theory, gauge theory, renormalization, photons, fermions, and related topics extend the same state-operator-spectrum logic to variable particle number, local fields, and scale-dependent descriptions.

\end{claimbox}

\section{Why This Branch Exists}

Field theory and gauge theory extend the same construction to many modes, local generators, and scale-dependent descriptions.

\label{page:photon}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Photon is a field-mode constructor: a one-quantum excitation of the electromagnetic field, constrained by massless dispersion and transverse polarization.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
The native photon mechanism is field quantization. The electromagnetic field is decomposed into modes, creation and annihilation operators act on those modes, and a one-photon state is created from the vacuum. The relevant readouts are occupation number, energy, momentum, polarization, and detector clicks; the constraints are dispersion and gauge-compatible transversality. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Photon contributes a many-mode field or particle-realization role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a many-mode or field-realization move: it extends the state and operator construction beyond a single-particle carrier.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. Fock space, field configuration space, or a sector selected by charge, spin, momentum, statistics, or gauge data.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Creation, annihilation, field, charge, spin, Hamiltonian, or scattering operators acting on the admissible sector.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Statistics, gauge constraints, commutation or anticommutation rules, domain conditions, and sector labels decide which states are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Occupation number, charge, spin, momentum, energy, correlation function, cross-section, or scattering amplitude.
\item \textbf{Check}. The field description must preserve the relevant observables under changes of representation and reduce to the expected particle or quasiparticle limit when that limit exists.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Topic-specific constructor: the equations express massless dispersion, one-mode occupation, number readout, and transverse polarization.
\begin{align*}
E=\hbar\omega,\qquad \mathbf p=\hbar\mathbf k,\qquad \omega=c|\mathbf k|\\
\ket{1_{\mathbf k,\lambda}}=a_{\mathbf k,\lambda}^{\dagger}\ket{0}\\
\hat N_{\mathbf k,\lambda}=a_{\mathbf k,\lambda}^{\dagger}a_{\mathbf k,\lambda}\\
\mathbf k\cdot\boldsymbol\epsilon_{\mathbf k,\lambda}=0
\end{align*}

\item the operator-to-spectrum relation: admissible observations are represented through eigenvalues, projections, modes, or outcome channels
\item the dependence of admissible readout on measurement context or boundary condition
\item the non-commuting compatibility structure, which survives changes of representation
\end{itemize}

\section{Fermion}
\label{page:fermion}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Fermion is an exchange-symmetry constructor: identical fermions live in antisymmetric sectors and obey Pauli exclusion.

The mechanism is an admissibility rule on many-body state space. Antisymmetry, anticommutation, and zero-or-one mode occupation define the portable role. \section{Boson}
\label{page:boson}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Boson is an exchange-symmetry constructor: identical bosons live in symmetric sectors and can share the same mode.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
The mechanism is symmetric exchange. Commuting creation and annihilation operators allow arbitrary nonnegative occupation of a mode, which supports field modes, coherent states, condensates, and photon-like readouts. \section{Quantum chromodynamics}
\label{page:quantum-chromodynamics}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Quantum chromodynamics can be read as a quantum construction: the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis fixes the admissible state space; a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, quantum chromodynamics is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or wave function, matrix or Hamiltonian, and eigenvalue or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through commutator or uncertainty relation. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Quantum chromodynamics contributes a lawful state-transport role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a many-mode or field-realization move: it extends the state and operator construction beyond a single-particle carrier.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. State terms: quantum state, wave function, or density operator. Context/domain terms: context and basis.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Operator terms: matrix. Protocol or update terms: projection.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Compatibility or closure terms: commutator, uncertainty relation, or non-commuting transformations. These determine which questions, states, or updates are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Readout terms: eigenvalue, energy level, or measurement outcome. These name the outcome labels, projectors, amplitudes, or records used for testing.
\item \textbf{Check}. A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
\begin{align*}
B \longmapsto \rho_B \quad \text{(context specifies an admissible state)}\\
\rho_t = U_t \rho_B U_t^\dagger \quad \text{(unitary evolution from preparation to readout)}\\
O = \sum_i \lambda_i P_i,\quad p_i=\operatorname{Tr}(P_i\rho_t) \quad \text{(spectral probability measure)}\\
[O_1,O_2]\neq 0 \quad \text{(incompatible observables: no common sharp basis)}
\end{align*}

\section{Fock space}
\label{page:fock-space}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Fock space is the occupation-number state space: the construction that replaces a fixed-particle Hilbert space by a direct sum over particle number.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
Fock space changes the carrier of the quantum state. Instead of describing one system in one Hilbert space, it builds sectors with zero, one, two, and more identical quanta, then imposes the bosonic or fermionic exchange rule. Creation and annihilation operators are the native coordinates of this page because they move the state between occupation sectors. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Fock space contributes a many-mode field or particle-realization role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as a many-mode or field-realization move: it extends the state and operator construction beyond a single-particle carrier.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. Fock space, field configuration space, or a sector selected by charge, spin, momentum, statistics, or gauge data.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Creation, annihilation, field, charge, spin, Hamiltonian, or scattering operators acting on the admissible sector.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Statistics, gauge constraints, commutation or anticommutation rules, domain conditions, and sector labels decide which states are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Occupation number, charge, spin, momentum, energy, correlation function, cross-section, or scattering amplitude.
\item \textbf{Check}. The field description must preserve the relevant observables under changes of representation and reduce to the expected particle or quasiparticle limit when that limit exists.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Topic-specific constructor: the equations express variable particle number, exchange symmetry, and occupation-number readout.
\begin{align*}
\mathcal F_{\pm}(\mathcal H)=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty} \mathcal S_{\pm}\mathcal H^{\otimes n}\\
[a_i,a_j^\dagger]_{\mp}=\delta_{ij},\qquad [a_i,a_j]_{\mp}=0\\
N=\sum_i a_i^\dagger a_i,\qquad N\ket{n_1,n_2,\ldots}=\left(\sum_i n_i\right)\ket{n_1,n_2,\ldots}
\end{align*}

\section{Quantum spacetime}
\label{page:quantum-spacetime}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
A quantum spacetime can be read as a quantum construction: the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis fixes the admissible state space; a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, a quantum spacetime is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or wave function, matrix or Hamiltonian, and eigenvalue or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through uncertainty relation or commutator. \section{Quantum channel}
\label{page:quantum-channel}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Quantum channel is the open-system protocol constructor: it maps input states to output states while preserving complete positivity and trace.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
A channel is the mechanism for noisy transformations, measurements with forgotten outcomes, and subsystem evolution. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Quantum channel contributes an engineered operation-sequence role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as an operation-sequence move: it specifies an ordered composition of allowed maps.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. Input and output density operators, possibly on different Hilbert spaces or subsystem carriers.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. A completely positive trace-preserving map, often represented by Kraus operators or by a Stinespring dilation.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Complete positivity and trace preservation are the legal conditions; non-trace-preserving maps require an explicitly conditioned outcome.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Output state, final POVM probabilities, fidelity, capacity, error rate, or recovered subsystem statistics.
\item \textbf{Check}. The channel claim requires a map that stays positive under extension by an untouched reference system and preserves total probability.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Standard constructor skeleton: completely positive trace-preserving map and readout.
\begin{align*}
\mathcal E(\rho)=\sum_a K_a\rho K_a^\dagger\\
\sum_aK_a^\dagger K_a=I\\
p(y)=\operatorname{Tr}(M_y\mathcal E(\rho))
\end{align*}

\section{Quantum logic gate}
\label{page:quantum-logic-gate}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
A quantum logic gate can be read as a quantum construction: the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis fixes the admissible state space; a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, a quantum logic gate is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or wave function, matrix or Hamiltonian, and eigenvalue or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through commutator or uncertainty relation. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Quantum logic gate contributes an engineered operation-sequence role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as an operation-sequence move: it specifies an ordered composition of allowed maps.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. State terms: quantum state. Context/domain terms: basis.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Operator terms: matrix, Hamiltonian, or unitary. Protocol or update terms: unitary evolution, projection or measurement update, or path integral weighting.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Compatibility or closure terms: commutator, uncertainty relation, or non-commuting transformations. These determine which questions, states, or updates are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Readout terms: eigenvalue, energy level, or measurement outcome. These name the outcome labels, projectors, amplitudes, or records used for testing.
\item \textbf{Check}. A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
\begin{align*}
B \longmapsto \rho_B \quad \text{(context specifies an admissible state)}\\
\rho_t = U_t \rho_B U_t^\dagger \quad \text{(unitary evolution from preparation to readout)}\\
O = \sum_i \lambda_i P_i,\quad p_i=\operatorname{Tr}(P_i\rho_t) \quad \text{(spectral probability measure)}\\
[O_1,O_2]\neq 0 \quad \text{(incompatible observables: no common sharp basis)}
\end{align*}

\section{Quantum circuit}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
Quantum circuit is the engineered-composition constructor: a finite sequence of admissible maps prepares, transforms, and measures a register.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
A circuit is the protocol layer of the same state-operator-readout machinery. Gates are controlled unitary or channel maps; measurement converts final states into output probabilities. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, normalization or admissibility; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure.

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Quantum circuit contributes an engineered operation-sequence role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as an operation-sequence move: it specifies an ordered composition of allowed maps.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. An input state, register, channel state, error syndrome, key, or controlled experimental configuration.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. An ordered sequence of gates, channels, measurements, corrections, encodings, or conditional maps.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Each step must belong to the claimed map class: unitary, completely positive, trace-preserving, projective, conditional, or corrective.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Output state, key, error rate, fidelity, channel capacity, algorithmic success probability, or sensor estimate.
\item \textbf{Check}. Changing operation order, inserting classical controls, or replacing a quantum channel should identify which step carries the effect.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
Standard constructor skeleton: composed gates and final measurement.

p(y)=\operatorname{Tr}(M_y\rho_{\mathrm{out}})
\end{align*}

\section{Quantum teleportation}
\label{page:quantum-teleportation}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
A quantum teleportation can be read as a quantum construction: the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis fixes the admissible state space; a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, a quantum teleportation is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or superposition, Hamiltonian or observable operator, and eigenvalue or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through commutator or uncertainty relation. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Quantum teleportation contributes a topic-native constructor role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as an operation-sequence move: it specifies an ordered composition of allowed maps.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. State terms: quantum state and superposition. Context/domain terms: basis.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Operator terms: Hamiltonian, observable operator, or generator. Protocol or update terms: collapse and projection.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Compatibility or closure terms: commutator, uncertainty relation, or non-commuting transformations. These determine which questions, states, or updates are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Readout terms: eigenvalue, energy level, or measurement outcome. These name the outcome labels, projectors, amplitudes, or records used for testing.
\item \textbf{Check}. A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
\begin{align*}
B \longmapsto \rho_B \quad \text{(context specifies an admissible state)}\\
\rho_t = U_t \rho_B U_t^\dagger \quad \text{(unitary evolution from preparation to readout)}\\
O = \sum_i \lambda_i P_i,\quad p_i=\operatorname{Tr}(P_i\rho_t) \quad \text{(spectral probability measure)}\\
[O_1,O_2]\neq 0 \quad \text{(incompatible observables: no common sharp basis)}
\end{align*}

\item the operator-to-spectrum relation: admissible observations are represented through eigenvalues, projections, modes, or outcome channels
\item the dependence of admissible readout on measurement context or boundary condition
\item the non-commuting compatibility structure, which survives changes of representation
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Annotations: history, interpretations, and popular frames}

\begin{claimbox}

Some pages help readers navigate the subject but do not form steps in the mechanism. They are kept as annotations so books, historical figures, interpretations, and popular frames do not distort the constructive tree.

\end{claimbox}

\section{Why This Branch Exists}

These pages remain useful for orientation and are placed downstream of the construction steps. This prevents biographies, books, and interpretations from becoming false roots of the mechanism.

\section{Mechanism Pages}

Each page below is read as a specialization of the compact quantum constructor. Topic-specific pages use explicit equations or overrides; core-derived pages use the branch-level equation form associated with their mechanism role.

\section{Introduction to quantum mechanics}
\label{page:introduction-to-quantum-mechanics}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
An introduction to quantum mechanics can be read as a quantum construction: the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis fixes the admissible state space; a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, an introduction to quantum mechanics is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or wave function, Hamiltonian or observable operator, and eigenvalue or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through uncertainty relation or complementarity. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Introduction to quantum mechanics contributes a topic-native constructor role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as an interpretive or historical move: it clarifies which formal layer is being discussed.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. State terms: quantum state, wave function, or density operator. Context/domain terms: basis.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Operator terms: Hamiltonian, observable operator, or generator. Protocol or update terms: Born rule.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Compatibility or closure terms: uncertainty and complementarity. These determine which questions, states, or updates are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Readout terms: eigenvalue, energy level, or measurement outcome. These name the outcome labels, projectors, amplitudes, or records used for testing.
\item \textbf{Check}. A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
\begin{align*}
B \longmapsto \rho_B \quad \text{(context specifies an admissible state)}\\
\rho_t = U_t \rho_B U_t^\dagger \quad \text{(unitary evolution from preparation to readout)}\\
O = \sum_i \lambda_i P_i,\quad p_i=\operatorname{Tr}(P_i\rho_t) \quad \text{(spectral probability measure)}\\
[O_1,O_2]\neq 0 \quad \text{(incompatible observables: no common sharp basis)}
\end{align*}

\subsection*{What Remains Stable}
\begin{itemize}
\item the rule connecting prepared states, observables, and spectral probability measures across wave, matrix, path-integral, circuit, or field notation
\item the operator-to-spectrum relation: admissible observations are represented through eigenvalues, projections, modes, or outcome channels
\item the dependence of admissible readout on measurement context or boundary condition
\item the non-commuting compatibility structure, which survives changes of representation
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{What Changes With Realization}
\begin{itemize}
\item the name of the carrier: particle, wave, field, qubit, or excitation
\item where time dependence is represented: on the state, on the operator, or in a path weight
\item the coordinate system, basis, or geometric picture used to display the same relation
\item the physical implementation of detector, boundary, preparation, or readout
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Checks}
\begin{itemize}
\item A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.
\item The validation criterion is that varying the context changes the admissible readout while the transformation law remains identifiable; shuffled or erased contexts should weaken the effect.
\end{itemize}

\section{Quantum mind}
\label{page:quantum-mind}
\par\smallskip\noindent\textit{This page is treated as historical, interpretive, or popular context rather than as a conceptual root.}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
a quantum mind modifies the interpretation of the probability/readout layer while preserving the formal quantum dynamics.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
a quantum mind acts on the readout layer of the quantum constructor. The formal ingredients remain the state assignment, the operator or measurement being applied, and the Born-rule map from projectors to probabilities. What changes is the status assigned to those ingredients: for this topic, the state or probability is treated through the agent, measurement context, or interpretive stance attached to the formalism. The page should therefore be read as a statement about the interpretation of state, probability, update, or recorded outcome while the Hamiltonian, spectral resolution, and commutator structure remain the formal reference layer. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout, state evolution, controlled update protocol; the strongest carrier signal is local notation, information profile, formula structure. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. \section{Modern Quantum Mechanics}
\label{page:modern-quantum-mechanics}
\par\smallskip\noindent\textit{This page is treated as historical, interpretive, or popular context rather than as a conceptual root.}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
A modern quantum mechanics can be read as a quantum construction: the potential, domain, initial condition, or boundary condition fixes the admissible state space; the Hamiltonian, whose exponential gives unitary time evolution defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, a modern Quantum Mechanics is described by a wave function or density operator defined on the Hilbert space allowed by the system's domain. The physical question is represented by the Hamiltonian, whose exponential gives unitary time evolution; the experimental or mathematical setting is the potential, domain, initial condition, or boundary condition. The observable content is obtained from the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of the relevant observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or wave function, Hamiltonian or observable operator, and eigenvalue or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection. In this page the compatibility condition is expressed through commutator or uncertainty relation. In the source-evidence profile for this page, the strongest construction signal is operator-to-spectrum readout,

\subsection*{Quantum Mechanism Frame}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Role}. Modern Quantum Mechanics contributes a topic-native constructor role to the quantum construction.
\item \textbf{Placement}. This page is read first as an interpretive or historical move: it clarifies which formal layer is being discussed.
\item \textbf{Carrier or domain}. State terms: quantum state, wave function, or density operator. Context/domain terms: preparation condition, measurement setup, or potential or domain.
\item \textbf{Operator or map}. Operator terms: Hamiltonian. Protocol or update terms: unitary evolution, projection or measurement update, or path integral weighting.
\item \textbf{Admissibility}. Compatibility or closure terms: commutator, uncertainty relation, or non-commuting transformations. These determine which questions, states, or updates are legal.
\item \textbf{Readout}. Readout terms: eigenvalue, energy level, or measurement outcome. These name the outcome labels, projectors, amplitudes, or records used for testing.
\item \textbf{Check}. A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Topic Equations}
\begin{align*}
B \longmapsto \rho_B \quad \text{(context specifies an admissible state)}\\
\rho_t = U_t \rho_B U_t^\dagger \quad \text{(unitary evolution from preparation to readout)}\\
O = \sum_i \lambda_i P_i,\quad p_i=\operatorname{Tr}(P_i\rho_t) \quad \text{(spectral probability measure)}\\
[O_1,O_2]\neq 0 \quad \text{(incompatible observables: no common sharp basis)}
\end{align*}

\subsection*{What Remains Stable}
\begin{itemize}
\item the rule connecting prepared states, observables, and spectral probability measures across wave, matrix, path-integral, circuit, or field notation
\item the operator-to-spectrum relation: admissible observations are represented through eigenvalues, projections, modes, or outcome channels
\item the dependence of admissible readout on measurement context or boundary condition
\item the non-commuting compatibility structure, which survives changes of representation
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{What Changes With Realization}
\begin{itemize}
\item the name of the carrier: particle, wave, field, qubit, or excitation
\item where time dependence is represented: on the state, on the operator, or in a path weight
\item the coordinate system, basis, or geometric picture used to display the same relation
\item the physical implementation of detector, boundary, preparation, or readout
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Checks}
\begin{itemize}
\item A concrete transfer target is a material, biological, or collective system with a state, a transformation, and a spectral or categorical readout, but without a tested incompatibility relation.
\item The validation criterion is that varying the context changes the admissible readout while the transformation law remains identifiable; shuffled or erased contexts should weaken the effect.
\end{itemize}

\section{The Quantum Universe}

\subsection*{Central Claim}
The quantum universe can be read as a quantum construction: the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis fixes the admissible state space; a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement defines the transformation or question; and spectral projectors with the Born rule determine the recorded probability distribution.

\subsection*{Formal Role}
In quantum-mechanical terms, The Quantum Universe is described by a two-dimensional Hilbert space, usually written as a qubit state or a density matrix. The physical question is represented by a Hamiltonian or unitary matrix rotating that state between preparation and measurement; the experimental or mathematical setting is the chosen basis, pulse sequence, or measurement axis. The observable content is obtained from projectors onto the two eigenstates of the measured observable. In the local terminology of this topic, the same construction appears through quantum state or wave function, Hamiltonian or observable operator, and eigenvalue or energy level. Probabilities enter only after this spectral decomposition: the Born rule assigns weights to projectors, not to informal object names. When two observables have a non-zero commutator, no single basis diagonalizes both; the limitation is therefore a statement about jointly available spectra, not about detector imperfection.
